Source slide page 2 (extracted text): Introduction to MATLAB ➢MATLAB is a programming language developed by MathWorks. ➢MATLAB stands for MATrix LABoratory. ➢MATLAB is a program for doing numerical computation. It was originally designed for solving linear algebra type problems using matrices. ➢While other programming languages mostly work with numbers one at a time, MATLAB is designed to operate primarily on whole matrices and arrays. EV0000113 · EV0000114 · EV0000115
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Introduction to MATLAB, Continued… ➢Using MATLAB, an image (or any other data like sound, etc.) can be converted to a matrix and then various operations can be performed on it to get the desired results and values. ➢MATLAB is a fourth-generation high-level programming language and interactive environment for numerical computation, visualization and programming. ➢It has numerous built-in commands and math functions that help in mathematical calculations, generating plots, and performing numerical methods. EV0000116 · EV0000117 · EV0000118
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): MATLAB's Power of Computational Mathematics ➢MATLAB is used in every fact of computational mathematics. Following are some commonly used mathematical calculations where MATLAB is used: • Dealing with Matrices and Arrays • 2-D and 3-D Plotting and graphics • Linear Algebra • Algebraic Equations • Statistics EV0000119 · EV0000120 · EV0000121
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): MATLAB's Power of Computational Mathematics, Continued… • Data Analysis • Calculus and Differential Equations • Numerical Calculations • Integration • Transforms • Curve Fitting • Special Functions EV0000122 · EV0000123 · EV0000124

Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Uses of MATLAB ➢MATLAB is widely used as a computational tool in Science and Engineering covering the fields of Physics, Chemistry, Mathematics, and all engineering streams. ➢It is used in a range of applications including: • Signal Processing and Communications • Algorithm development • Control Systems • Computational Finance; Computational Biology EV0000125 · EV0000126 · EV0000127

Source slide page 15 (extracted text): MATLAB Screen • Command Window • type commands • Current Directory • View folders and m-files • Workspace • View program variables • Double click on a variable to see it in the Array Editor • Command History • view past commands • save a whole session using diary 15 EV0000152 · EV0000153 · EV0000154
  16. Source slide page 16 (extracted text): Hands on Practice ➢MATLAB environment behaves like a super-complex calculator. We can enter commands at the >> command prompt. ➢In MATLAB, we give a command and it executes the command right away. ➢Type a valid expression, for example, >>20 + 21; and press ENTER. ➢ When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is ans=41. EV0000155 · EV0000156 · EV0000157
  17. Source slide page 17 (extracted text): Hands on Practice, Continued… ➢Let us take up few more examples: ➢>>3 ^ 2 (%3 raised to the power of 2). When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is ans=9. ➢>>sin(pi/2). (% sine of angle 90). When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is ans=1. ➢>>7/0 (% Divide by zero). When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is ans=Inf. EV0000158 · EV0000159 · EV0000160
  18. Source slide page 18 (extracted text): Hands on Practice, Continued… ➢>>732 * 20.3. When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is ans=1.4860e+04. ➢MATLAB provides some special expressions for some mathematical symbols, like pi for 𝜋, Inf for ∞, 𝑖 (and 𝑗) for −1 . ➢In MATLAB, NaN stands for 'not a number'. For example, >> 0/0; -inf/inf EV0000161 · EV0000162 · EV0000163
  19. Source slide page 19 (extracted text): Use of Semicolon (;) in MATLAB ➢Semicolon (;) indicates end of statement. However, if we want to suppress and hide the MATLAB output for an expression, add a semicolon after the expression. ➢For example, >>x = 5; y = x + 5. When we click the Execute button, MATLAB executes it immediately and the result returned is y=8. EV0000164 · EV0000165 · EV0000166
  20. Source slide page 20 (extracted text): Adding Comments in MATLAB ➢To add comments to MATLAB code, we use the percent ( % ) symbol. Comment lines can appear anywhere in a program file, and we can add comments to the end of a line of code. For example, y = sum(x) % Use the sum function EV0000167 · EV0000168 · EV0000169
  21. Source slide page 21 (extracted text): Commonly used Operators and Special Characters Operation Symbol Example Addition + 5+3=8 Subtraction - 5-3=2 Multiplication * 5*3=15 Right Division / 5/3=1.6667 Left Division \ 5/3=3\5=1.667 Exponentiation ^ 5^3=125 EV0000170 · EV0000171 · EV0000172
  22. Source slide page 22 (extracted text): Special Variables and Constants MATLAB supports the following special variables and constants: EV0000173 · EV0000174 · EV0000175
  23. Source slide page 23 (extracted text): Naming Variables ➢Variable names consist of a letter followed by any number of letters, digits or underscore. ➢MATLAB is case-sensitive. ➢For example, >>x = 10 (% defining x and initializing it with a value). MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result x = 10. ➢>>x = sqrt(25) (% defining x and initializing it with an expression) MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the result as x = 5. EV0000176 · EV0000177 · EV0000178
  24. Source slide page 24 (extracted text): Naming Variables, Continued… ➢Note that once a variable is entered into the system, we can refer to it later. Variables must have values before they are used. When an expression returns a result that is not assigned to any variable, the system assigns it to a variable named answer, which can be used later. ➢For example, >>x = 7 * 8; y = x * 7.89. MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result y = 441.8400. EV0000179 · EV0000180 · EV0000181
  25. Source slide page 25 (extracted text): Multiple assignments ➢We can have multiple assignments on the same line. ➢For example, >> a = 2; b = 7; c = a * b ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the result c = 14. EV0000182 · EV0000183 · EV0000184
  26. Source slide page 26 (extracted text): I have forgotten the Variables ➢The who command displays all the variable names we have used. ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the result: Your variables are: ans - - - … ➢The whos command. MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result. EV0000185 · EV0000186 · EV0000187
  27. Source slide page 27 (extracted text): I have forgotten the Variables >> clear x. It will delete x, won't display anything. >> clear. It will delete all variables in the workspace. >> clc. It clears all the text from the Command Window, resulting in a clear screen. EV0000188 · EV0000189 · EV0000190
  28. Source slide page 28 (extracted text): Long Assignments ➢Long assignments can be extended to another line as follows: >> initial_velocity = 0; >> acceleration = 9.8; >> time = 20; >> Final_velocity = initial_velocity + acceleration * time ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: final velocity = 196. EV0000191 · EV0000192 · EV0000193
  29. Source slide page 29 (extracted text): The format Command ➢By default, MATLAB displays numbers with four decimal place values. This is known as short format. ➢However, if we want more precision, then we need to use the format command. ➢The format long command displays 15 digits after the decimal point. ➢For example: >> format long >> x = 7 + 10/3 + 5 ^ 1.2 ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: x = 17.231981640639408. EV0000194 · EV0000195 · EV0000196
  30. Source slide page 30 (extracted text): The format Command, Continued… Another example, >> format short >> x = 7 + 10/3 + 5 ^ 1.2 ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: x = 17.232 ➢The format bank command rounds numbers to two decimal places. EV0000197 · EV0000198 · EV0000199
  31. Source slide page 31 (extracted text): The format Command, Continued… For example, >> format bank >> daily_wage = 177.45; weekly_wage = daily_wage * 6 ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: weekly_wage = 1064.70 ➢MATLAB displays large numbers using exponential notation. ➢The format short e command allows displaying in exponential form with four decimal places plus the exponent. EV0000200 · EV0000201 · EV0000202
  32. Source slide page 32 (extracted text): The format Command, Continued… ➢For example, >> format short e >> 4.678 * 4.9 ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: ans = 2.2922e+01 ➢The format long e command allows displaying in exponential form with fifteen decimal places plus the exponent. EV0000203 · EV0000204 · EV0000205
  33. Source slide page 33 (extracted text): The format Command, Continued… ➢For example, >> format long e >> x = pi ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: x = 3.141592653589793e+00 ➢The format rat command gives the closest rational expression resulting from a calculation. ➢For example, >> format rat >> 4.678 * 4.9 ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: ans = 34177/1491 EV0000206 · EV0000207 · EV0000208
  34. Source slide page 34 (extracted text): Creating Vectors ➢A vector is a one-dimensional array of numbers. ➢MATLAB allows creating two types of vectors: ➢Row vectors and Column vectors. ➢Row vectors are created by enclosing the set of elements in square brackets, using space or comma. ➢For example, >> X = [7 8 9 10 11]. MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: X = 7 8 9 10 11 EV0000209 · EV0000210 · EV0000211
  35. Source slide page 35 (extracted text): Creating Vectors, Continued… ➢Another example, >> X = [7 8 9 10 11]; >> Y = [2, 3, 4, 5, 6]; >> Z = X +Y ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: Z = 9 11 13 15 17 EV0000212 · EV0000213 · EV0000214
  36. Source slide page 36 (extracted text): Creating Vectors, Continued… ➢Column vectors are created by enclosing the set of elements in square brackets, using semicolon(;). ➢For example, >> X = [7; 8; 9; 10] ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: X = 7 8 9 10 EV0000215 · EV0000216 · EV0000217
  37. Source slide page 37 (extracted text): Creating matrices ➢A matrix is a two-dimensional array of numbers. ➢In MATLAB, a matrix is created by entering each row as a sequence of space or comma separated elements, and end of a row is terminated by a semicolon. ➢For example, let us create a 3-by-3 matrix as >>A = [1 2 3 4; 4 5 6 7; 7 8 9 10] EV0000218 · EV0000219 · EV0000220
  38. Source slide page 38 (extracted text): Creating matrices, Continued… ➢MATLAB will execute the above statement and return the following result: A = 1 2 3 4 4 5 6 7 7 8 9 10 EV0000221 · EV0000222 · EV0000223
  39. Source slide page 39 (extracted text): Elementary Math Built – In Functions Function Description Examples sqrt(x) Square root >>sqrt(81) ans=9 nthroot(x,n) Real nth root of a real number x. (If x is negative n >>nthroot(8,3) must be an odd integer). ans=2 exp(x) Exponential(e^x) >>exp(5) ans=1.484131591025766e+02 abs(x) Absolute value >>abs(-24) ans=24 log(x) Natural logarithm. Base e logarithm (ln) >>log(1000) ans=6.907755278982137e+00 log10(x) Base 10 logarithm >>log10(1000) ans=3 factorial(x) The factorial function x! >>factorial(5) (x must be a psitive integer) ans=120 EV0000224 · EV0000225 · EV0000226
  40. Source slide page 40 (extracted text): Trigonometric Math Functions Function Description Examples sin(x) Sine of angle x ( x in radians) >>sin(pi/6) ans=0.5000 sind(x) Sine of angle x (x in degrees) >>sind(30) ans=0.5000 cos(x) Cosine of angle x (x in radians) >>cos(0.5) ans=0.8776 cosd(x) Cosine of angle x (x in degrees) >>cosd(30) ans=0.8660 tan(x) Tangent of angle x (x in radians) >>tan(pi/6) ans=0.5774 tand(x) Tangent of angle x (x in degrees) >>tand(45) ans=1 cotx) Cotangent of angle x (x in radians) >>cot(0.5) ans=1.8305 cotd(x) Cotangent of angle x ( x in degrees) >>cotd(90) ans=1 asin(x) Inverse of sine of angle x (x in radians) >>asin(0.5) ans=0.5236 asind(x) Inverse of sine angle x ( x in degrees) >>asin(0.8660) ans=59.9971 acos(x) Inverse of cosine of angle x (x in radians) >>acos(0.3) ans=1.2661 acosd(x) Inverse of cosine angle x ( x in degrees) >>acosd(0.5) ans=60 EV0000227 · EV0000228 · EV0000229
  41. Source slide page 41 (extracted text): Rounding Functions Function Description Examples round(x) Round to the nearest integer >>round(25/3) ans=8 >>round(4.49) ans=4 >>round(4.5) ans=5 >>round(4.9999) ans=5 fix(x) Round towards zero. In other words, chops off the >>fix(17/5) ans=3 fraction part. >>fix(4.49) ans=4 >>fix(4.9999) ans=4 >>fix(-4.6674) ans=-4 ceil(x) Round towards positive infinity. >>ceil(27/2) ans=14 >>ceil(2.1) ans=3 >>ceil(2.9) ans=3 >>ceil(-4.99) ans=-4 floor(x) Round towards minus infinity >>floor(-9/4) ans=-3 >>floor(2.1) ans=2 >>floor(2.99) ans=2 rem(x,y) Returns the remainder after x is divided by y >>rem(13,5) ans=3 EV0000230 · EV0000231 · EV0000232

Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Integers ➢An integer is a whole number (not a fraction) that can be positive, negative, or zero. ➢For example, the numbers 10, 0, and -25 are integers. ➢When two integers are added, subtracted, or multiplied, the result is also an integer. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Floating Point Numbers ➢As the name indicates, floating point numbers are numbers that contain floating decimal points. ➢For example, the numbers 6.5, 0.0001, and -2,345.6789 are floating point numbers. ➢When a calculation includes a floating point number, it is called a "floating point calculation."   9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): Character ➢Single alphanumeric symbol enclosed in a single quote is a character constant. ➢Example, ‘B’ and ‘6’. ➢6 and ‘6’ are different. Here, 6 is numeric constant and ‘6’ is character constant. EV0000261 · EV0000262 · EV0000263
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): Strings ➢Any two or more alphanumeric symbols enclosed in a single quote. ➢Example, ‘INDIA’=[‘I’, ‘N’, ‘D’, ‘I’, ‘A’] EV0000264 · EV0000265 · EV0000266

Source slide page 19 (extracted text): Logical Operators: ➢The logical data type represents true or false states using the numbers 1 and 0, respectively. ➢The three logical operators are &; |; and ~ ➢The meaning of & operator is AND . ➢The meaning of | operator is OR ➢ The meaning of ~ operator is NOT EV0000291 · EV0000292 · EV0000293

  24. Source slide page 24 (extracted text): Logical Operator |, Continued… Consider, >>a=[1 1 1 0 0 0]; b=[0 0 0 1 1 1]; >> a|b ans = . 1×6 logical array 1 1 1 1 1 1 EV0000306 · EV0000307 · EV0000308
  25. Source slide page 25 (extracted text): Logical Operator ~ : It complements each element of input array, A. The truth table of ~ is as follows: operand NOT operand 0 1 0 1 EV0000309 · EV0000310 · EV0000311

4M-Files; Loop Control Statements; Conditional Statements

Important points and why

  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS-I MATLAB Department of Science and Humanities EV0000314 · EV0000315 · EV0000316
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): The M-Files: ➢In MATLAB, we write programs in M-files. ➢They are called M-files because they must have a .m at the end of their name, (for example, myfunction.m). ➢M-files can be created using any editor or word processing applications. ➢There are two types of M-files: Script files and Function files. EV0000317 · EV0000318 · EV0000319
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): Script Files; Creating and Running Script Files ➢A script file is an M-file with a set of valid MATLAB commands in it. ➢To create scripts files, we need to use a text editor. We can open the MATLAB editor using the command prompt. ➢If we use the command prompt, then we type edit and then the filename (with .m extension). This will open the editor. ➢The above command will create the file in default MATLAB directory. ➢Alternatively, we can choose NEW -> Script. This also opens the editor and creates a file named untitled. We can name and save the file after typing the code. EV0000320 · EV0000321 · EV0000322
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): Creating and Running Script Files, Continued… ➢ Consider an example: ➢ Type the following code in the editor: >> a=25; b=26; c=27; d=a+b+c ➢After creating and saving the file, you can run it by clicking the Run button on the editor window. ➢The command window prompt displays the result: d=78 EV0000323 · EV0000324 · EV0000325
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Function Files ➢A function is a group of statements that together perform a task. ➢In MATLAB, functions are defined in separate files. ➢Functions can accept more than one input arguments and may return more than one output arguments. ➢The syntax of the function definition line is as follows: function [output variables] = function_name(input variables) ➢Note that the function_name must be the same as the file name (without the m.extension) in which the function is written. EV0000326 · EV0000327 · EV0000328
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): Function Files, Continued… ➢Consider the following example: ➢First, create a script file as myname.m for the following: >> a=20; b=21; c=22; >> sum=a+b+c (Press Run icon) >> prod=a*b*c (Press Run icon) ➢Then the following result is displayed on the command window: sum = 63; prod =9240. EV0000329 · EV0000330 · EV0000331
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): Function Files, Continued… Create a function file: >> function [sum,prod] = myname(a,b,c) >> sum = a+b+c; >> prod=a*b*c; >> end On command window, type the following: For, >> myname(20,21,22), the output is: ans =63 For, >> [s,p]=myname(20,21,22), the output is: s = 63, p =9240 EV0000332 · EV0000333 · EV0000334
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): Loop Control Statements ➢With loop control statements, we can repeatedly execute a block of code. There are two types of loops: for loops and while loops. ➢for loop: A for loop is used to repeat a statement or a group of statements for a fixed number of times. ➢The syntax is: for index = values <program statements> ……………………………… end EV0000335 · EV0000336 · EV0000337
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): for loop, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following code: for m=1:3 num=1/(m+1) end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: num = 0.5000 num = 0.3333 num = 0.2500 EV0000338 · EV0000339 · EV0000340
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): for loop, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following code: for n=100:-2:96 k=1/(exp(n)) end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: k =3.7201e-44 k =2.7488e-43 k =2.0311e-42 EV0000341 · EV0000342 · EV0000343
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): for loop, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following code: >> for a = 1:-0.1: 0 >> disp(a) >> end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: 1 0.9000 0.8000 0.7000 0.6000 0.5000 0.4000 0.3000 0.2000 0.1000 0 (These numbers will be displayed as a column matrix) EV0000344 · EV0000345 · EV0000346
  12. Source slide page 12 (extracted text): while loop ➢The while loop repeatedly executes statements while condition is true. ➢The while loop repeatedly executes program statement(s) as long as the expression remains true. ➢The syntax is: while <expression> <program statements> ……………………………… end EV0000347 · EV0000348 · EV0000349
  13. Source slide page 13 (extracted text): while loop, Continued… ➢Consider the following example: The code is as follows: >> a = 10; >> while(a < 15) >> fprintf('value of a: %d\n', a); >> a = a + 1; >> end EV0000350 · EV0000351 · EV0000352
  14. Source slide page 14 (extracted text): while loop, Continued… ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: value of a: 10 value of a: 11 value of a: 12 value of a: 13 value of a: 14 EV0000353 · EV0000354 · EV0000355
  15. Source slide page 15 (extracted text): Conditional Statements ➢There will be some situation where a program or a particular block has to be executed only when a specific condition is true. The conditional statements will be useful in such situations. ➢The following are the conditional statements that we can use in MATLAB. if-end; if-else-end; if-elseif-elseif-else-end; switch case. EV0000356 · EV0000357 · EV0000358
  16. Source slide page 16 (extracted text): if-end statement ➢if-end statement: It decides whether a particular block of code has to be executed or not, based on the given Boolean condition. When the given condition is true, only then it executes the statements inside the block otherwise not. EV0000359 · EV0000360 · EV0000361
  17. Source slide page 17 (extracted text): if-end statement, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following: >> number = 25; >> if number > 20 >> fprintf('The number is greater than 20'); >> end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: The number is greater than 20. EV0000362 · EV0000363 · EV0000364
  18. Source slide page 18 (extracted text): if-end statement, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following: >> number = 50; >> if number > 60 >> fprintf('The number is greater than 60'); >> end ➢When we run the file, no result will be displayed on the screen. EV0000365 · EV0000366 · EV0000367
  19. Source slide page 19 (extracted text): if-else-end statement ➢An if statement can be followed by an optional else statement, which executes when the expression is false. Consider the following: >> number = 25; >> if number<20 >> fprintf('The number is greater than 20'); else >> fprintf('The number is not less than 20'); >> end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: The number is not less than 20. EV0000368 · EV0000369 · EV0000370
  20. Source slide page 20 (extracted text): if-elseif-elseif-else-end ➢We can use chain if-else-end statements with more than one condition. For example, consider the following: >> number = 50; >> if number<20 >> fprintf('The number is less than 20\n'); >> else >> if number<30 >> fprintf('The number is less than 30\n'); >> else >> fprintf('The number is less than 60\n'); >> end >> end ➢ Output: The number is less than 60. EV0000371 · EV0000372 · EV0000373
  21. Source slide page 21 (extracted text): Switch statement ➢The switch statement is used to test for the equality against a set of known values. ➢The syntax for switch statement is: switch <switch_expression> case <case_expression> <statements> case <case_expression> <statements> ……………………………………. otherwise <statements> end EV0000374 · EV0000375 · EV0000376
  22. Source slide page 22 (extracted text): Switch statement, Continued… ➢For example, consider the following: >> grade = 'B'; >> switch(grade) >> case 'A' >> fprintf('Excellent!\n' ); >> case 'B' >> fprintf('Well done\n' ); >> case 'C' >> fprintf('Well done\n' ); >> case 'D' >> fprintf('You passed\n' ); EV0000377 · EV0000378 · EV0000379
  23. Source slide page 23 (extracted text): Switch statement, Continued… >> case 'F' >> fprintf('Better try again\n' ); >> otherwise >> fprintf('Invalid grade\n' ); >> end ➢When we run the file, it displays the following result: Well done ## Page / slide 24 (OCR) THANK YOU PES UNIVERSITY EV0000380 · EV0000381 · EV0000382

The smaller the step size, the smoother the displayed curve usually becomes.   19. Source slide page 19 (extracted text): 19. Title and Axis Labels Good graphs tell the reader what is being plotted. Always label axes and give the graph a meaningful title. Try it: Label a graph of y = x^2 with units if appropriate. x = 0:0.01:2*pi; y = sin(x); plot(x,y) xlabel('x') ylabel('sin(x)') title('Sine Curve') grid on 19 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000526 · EV0000527 · EV0000528
  20. Source slide page 20 (extracted text): 20. Grid, Legend & Line Properties Common plot properties include line color, line width and line style. A legend identifies curves when more than one curve is present. Try it: Try '--' for a dashed line. x = 0:0.01:2*pi; plot(x,sin(x),'b','LineWidth',1.5) grid on xlabel('x') ylabel('y') title('Sine Curve') legend('sin(x)') 20 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000529 · EV0000530 · EV0000531
  21. Source slide page 21 (extracted text): 21. Multiple Curves in One Plot hold on lets you add another curve to the same axes. hold off returns MATLAB to the normal plotting behavior. Try it: Add y = sin(2x) to the same graph. x = 0:0.01:2*pi; y1 = sin(x); y2 = cos(x); plot(x,y1,'LineWidth',1.5) hold on plot(x,y2,'LineWidth',1.5) hold off grid on legend('sin(x)','cos(x)') xlabel('x') ylabel('Value') title('Sine and Cosine') 21 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000532 · EV0000533 · EV0000534
  22. Source slide page 22 (extracted text): 22. Curve Labelling & Editing MATLAB Figure tools allow interactive editing. You can inspect and modify plot properties, labels, axes and curves without rewriting everything. Try it: Open the Figure window and identify the plot editing tools. x = 0:0.01:2*pi; y = sin(x); plot(x,y,'LineWidth',1.5) grid on xlabel('x') ylabel('y') title('Interactive Curve Editing') % You can also use the Figure % window tools to edit labels, % lines and axes. 22 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000535 · EV0000536 · EV0000537
  23. Source slide page 23 (extracted text): 23. 2D Polar Plot Polar coordinates use radius r and angle θ instead of x and y. polarplot(theta,r) is designed for polar graphs. Try it: Try r = 2*cos(theta). What shape do you get? theta = 0:0.01:2*pi; r = 1 + cos(theta); polarplot(theta,r) title('Cardioid') 23 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000538 · EV0000539 · EV0000540
  24. Source slide page 24 (extracted text): 24. Cartesian vs Polar Plot Cartesian Polar • Coordinates: (x, y) • Horizontal and vertical axes • Use plot(x,y) • Useful for y = f(x) • Coordinates: (r, θ) • Radius and angle • Use polarplot(θ,r) • Useful for circular/radial patterns 24 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000541 · EV0000542 · EV0000543
  25. Source slide page 25 (extracted text): 25. Introduction to 3D Plotting A 3D curve uses x, y and z coordinates. plot3 creates a three-dimensional line plot. Try it: Rotate the Figure using the interactive 3D controls. t = 0:0.01:10*pi; x = cos(t); y = sin(t); z = t; plot3(x,y,z) grid on xlabel('x') ylabel('y') zlabel('z') title('3D Helix') 25 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000544 · EV0000545 · EV0000546
  26. Source slide page 26 (extracted text): 26. 3D Cartesian Curve Parametric curves define x, y and z using a common parameter t. Changing the equations changes the shape of the curve. Try it: Change z = t/3 to z = t/6 and observe the effect. t = 0:0.02:20; x = cos(t); y = sin(t); z = t/3; plot3(x,y,z,'LineWidth',1.5) grid on xlabel('x') ylabel('y') zlabel('z') title('3D Parametric Curve') 26 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000547 · EV0000548 · EV0000549
  27. Source slide page 27 (extracted text): 27. 3D Surface Plot A surface is defined over a grid of X and Y values. meshgrid creates the coordinate grid and surf displays the surface. Try it: Try Z = sin(sqrt(X.^2 + Y.^2)). [X,Y] = meshgrid(-5:0.2:5); Z = X.^2 + Y.^2; surf(X,Y,Z) shading interp grid on xlabel('X') ylabel('Y') zlabel('Z') title('Paraboloid Surface') 27 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000550 · EV0000551 · EV0000552
  28. Source slide page 28 (extracted text): 28. 3D Polar / Cylindrical Visualization MATLAB can convert polar/cylindrical coordinates into Cartesian coordinates for 3D visualization. x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ. Try it: Change 0.2 to 0.5 and observe the shape. theta = 0:0.05:2*pi; r = 1 + 0.2*cos(3*theta); x = r.*cos(theta); y = r.*sin(theta); z = zeros(size(theta)); plot3(x,y,z,'LineWidth',2) grid on xlabel('x') ylabel('y') zlabel('z') title('Polar Curve in 3D Axes') 28 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000553 · EV0000554 · EV0000555
  29. Source slide page 29 (extracted text): 29. Mini Hands-on Exercise Task A – 2D Task B – 3D • Plot y = x² for -5 ≤ x ≤ 5. • Add title, x-label, y-label and grid. • Change the line width. • Add a legend. • Create a 3D helix. • Label x, y and z axes. • Add a title and grid. • Rotate the graph and observe it from different views. 29 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000556 · EV0000557 · EV0000558
  30. Source slide page 30 (extracted text): 30. Summary & Practice Tasks • MATLAB provides an interactive environment for engineering computation. • Variables, vectors and matrices form the foundation of MATLAB. • plot and polarplot create 2D graphs; plot3 and surf create 3D visualizations. • Use xlabel, ylabel, zlabel, title, legend and grid on to improve graphs. • Practice by changing parameters and predicting how the graph will change. Practice: plot y = sin(x), y = cos(x), a polar cardioid, a 3D helix and a 3D surface. 30 Hands-on Learning Using MATLAB • First-Year B.Tech EV0000559 · EV0000560 · EV0000561

Graphs and diagrams

Important points and why

  1. Source slide page 1 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS-I MATLAB Department of Science and Humanities EV0000384 · EV0000385 · EV0000386
  2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): MATLAB – Plotting ➢To plot the graph of a function, we need to take the following steps: ➢Define x, by specifying the range of values for the variable x, for which the function is to be plotted. ➢Define the function, y = f(x). Call the plot command, as plot(x, y). ➢The plot command is used to create two-dimensional plots. ➢Following example would demonstrate the concept. ➢Let us plot the function 𝑦= 3.5−0.5𝑥cos 6𝑥 for −2 ≤𝑥≤4. EV0000387 · EV0000388 · EV0000389
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): MATLAB – Plotting, Continued… ➢The script file is as follows: >> x=[-2:0.01:4]; (Here 0.01 is the spacing value) >> y=3.5.^(-0.5*x).*cos(6*x); >> plot(x,y) ➢Here 𝑦is plotted as a function of 𝑥. EV0000390 · EV0000391 · EV0000392
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): MATLAB – Plotting, Continued… ➢When we run the file, MATLAB displays the following plot: EV0000393 · EV0000394 · EV0000395
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): MATLAB – Plotting, Continued… ➢Let us take another example to plot the function 𝑦= 𝑥2. ➢In this example, we will draw two graphs with the same function, but in second time, we will reduce the value of increment. ➢Note that as we decrease the increment, the graph becomes smoother. ➢The script file is as follows: >> x = [-100:20:100]; >> y = x.^2; >> plot(x, y) EV0000396 · EV0000397 · EV0000398
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): MATLAB – Plotting, Continued… ➢When we run the file, MATLAB displays the following plot: EV0000399 · EV0000400 · EV0000401

  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): MATLAB - Three Dimensional Plots ➢Three-dimensional plots display a surface defined by a function in two variables, 𝑔= 𝑓(𝑥, 𝑦). ➢To define the function 𝑔= 𝑓(𝑥, 𝑦), we first create a set of 𝑥, 𝑦 points over the domain of the function using the meshgrid command. If the radius is increased by 0.01 m and length by 0.05m, find the percentage change in volume of balloon. syms r h dr dh; V = pi * r^2 * h + (2/3) * pi * r^3; dV = diff(V, r) * dr + diff(V, h) * dh; r_val = 1.5; % meters h_val = 4; % meters dr_val = 0.01; % meters dh_val = 0.05; % meters V_value = double(subs(V, {r, h}, {r_val, h_val})); dV_value = double(subs(dV, {r, h, dr, dh}, {r_val, h_val, dr_val, dh_val})); percentage_change = (dV_value / V_value) * 100 Output EV0000685 · EV0000686 · EV0000687

Source slide page 5 (extracted text): Partial Derivative-Class II 4) Divide 24 into 3 parts such that the continued product of the first, square of the second and the cube of the third may be maximum. syms x y z lambda L = x * y^2 * z^3 + lambda * (24 - x - y - z); eq1 = diff(L, x) == 0; eq2 = diff(L, y) == 0; eq3 = diff(L, z) == 0; eq4 = diff(L, lambda) == 0; sol = solve([eq1, eq2, eq3, eq4], [x, y, z, lambda], 'Real', true); x_vals = double(sol.x); y_vals = double(sol.y); z_vals = double(sol.z); for i = 1:length(x_vals) if x_vals(i) > 0 && y_vals(i) > 0 && z_vals(i) > 0 x_sol = x_vals(i) y_sol = y_vals(i) z_sol = z_vals(i) break; end end max_product = x_sol * y_sol^2 * z_sol^3 Output:   x_sol = 4 y_sol = 8 z_sol = 12 max_product = 442368 EV0000688 · EV0000689 · EV0000690

  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS-I MATLAB S = dsolve(eqn) solves the differential equation eqn, where eqn is a symbolic equation. Use diff and == to represent differential equations. For example, diff(y,x) == y  represents the equation dy/dx = y. Solve a system of differential equations by specifying eqn as a vector of those equations. S = dsolve(eqn,cond) solves eqn with the initial or boundary condition cond. [y1,...,yN] = dsolve(___) assigns the solutions to the variables y1,...,yN. EV0000727 · EV0000728 · EV0000729
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS-I MATLAB Syntax for dsolve: S = dsolve(eqn) S = dsolve(eqn,cond) [y1,...,yN] = dsolve(___) EV0000730 · EV0000731 · EV0000732

  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): Separation of Variables = = (equation) Symbolic eq object | PDE = diff(u, t) = = diff(u, x, 2) | Creates equation, Not assignment subs substitution | Subs(PDE, u, X(x)*T(t)) | Replaces u with X(x)*T(t) EV0000892 · EV0000893 · EV0000894
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): %% Step 1: Define symbolic variables syms x t k lambda u(x, t) X(x) T(t) %% Step 2: Define the PDE PDE = (1/k) * diff(u, t) == diff(u, x, 2); %% Step 3: Using separation of Variables u(x, t) = X(x) * T(t); %% Step 4: Substituting u(x,t) and dividing the PDE with u(x,t) PDE_sep = subs(PDE, u, X(x)*T(t)); PDE_sep = PDE_sep / (X(x) * T(t)); %% Step 5: Separating the two ODE's obtained (1/k) * diff(T, t) / T == diff(X, x, 2) / X; eqT = (1/k) * diff(T, t) == lambda * T; eqX = diff(X, x, 2) == lambda * X; %% Step 6: Solving the two ODE's T_sol = dsolve(eqT) X_sol = dsolve(eqX) %% Step 7: Combining the two solutions u_sol = simplify(X_sol * T_sol) EV0000895 · EV0000896 · EV0000897

Source slide page 13 (extracted text): Write code for solution of PDE using Separation of Variables %% Step 1: Define symbolic variables syms x y lambda u(x, y) X(x) Y(y) %% Step 2: Define the PDE PDE = 3 * diff(u, x) + 2 * diff(u, y) == 0; %% Step 3: Using separation of Variables u(x, y) = X(x) * Y(y); %% Step 4: Substituting u(x,y) and dividing the PDE with u(x,y) PDE_sep = subs(PDE, u, X(x) * Y(y)); PDE_sep = PDE_sep / (X(x) * Y(y)); %% Step 5: Separating the two ODE's obtained - 2 * diff(Y, y) / Y == 3 * diff(X, x) / X; eqY = - 2 * diff(Y, y) == lambda * Y; eqX = 3 * diff(X, x) == lambda * X; %% Step 6: Solving the two ODE's Y_sol = dsolve(eqY) X_sol = dsolve(eqX) %% Step 7: Combining the two solutions u_sol = simplify(X_sol * Y_sol) EV0000901 · EV0000902 · EV0000903

Source slide page 15 (extracted text): Write code for solution of PDE using Separation of Variables %% Step 1: Define symbolic variables syms x t lambda u(x, t) X(x) T(t) %% Step 2: Define the PDE PDE = 5 * diff(u, x) - 4 * diff(u, t) == 0; %% Step 3: Using separation of Variables u(x, t) = X(x) * T(t); %% Step 4: Substituting u(x,t) and dividing the PDE with u(x,t) PDE_sep = subs(PDE, u, X(x) * T(t)); PDE_sep = PDE_sep / (X(x) * T(t)); %% Step 5: Separating the two ODE's obtained 5 * diff(X, x) / X == 4 * diff(T, t) / T; eqX = 5 * diff(X, x) == lambda * X; eqT = 4 * diff(T, t) == lambda * T; %% Step 6: Solving the two ODE's X_sol = dsolve(eqX) T_sol = dsolve(eqT) %% Step 7: Combining the two solutions u_sol = simplify(X_sol * T_sol) EV0000907 · EV0000908 · EV0000909

Source slide page 17 (extracted text): %% Step 1: Define symbolic variables syms x y lambda u(x, y) X(x) Y(y) %% Step 2: Define the PDE PDE = diff(u, x, 2) - 2 * diff(u, x) + diff(u, y) == 0; %% Step 3: Using separation of Variables u(x, y) = X(x) * Y(y); %% Step 4: Substituting u(x,y) and dividing the PDE with u(x,y) PDE_sep = subs(PDE, u, X(x) * Y(y)); PDE_sep = PDE_sep / (X(x) * Y(y)); %% Step 5: Separating the two ODE's obtained - diff(Y, y) / Y == diff(X, x, 2) - 2 * diff(X, x) / X; eqY = diff(Y, y) == -lambda * Y; eqX = diff(X, x, 2) - 2 * diff(X, x) == lambda * X; %% Step 6: Solving the two ODE's Y_sol = dsolve(eqY) X_sol = dsolve(eqX) %% Step 7: Combining the two solutions u_sol = simplify(X_sol * Y_sol) EV0000913 · EV0000914 · EV0000915

Source slide page 14 (extracted text): One-Dimensional Heat equation syms u(x, t) x t % Symbolic variables and functions PDE = diff(u, t) == diff(u, x, 2); syms X(x) T(t) u(x, t) = X(x) * T(t); % Assume u(x,t) = X(x)T(t) PDE_sep = subs(PDE, u, X(x)*T(t)); % Substitute into the PDE PDE_sep = PDE_sep / (X(x) * T(t)); eq1 = diff(T, t) / T == diff(X, x, 2) / X; % Separate variables syms lambda eqT = diff(T, t) == -lambda * T; % Set equal to separation constant -lambda eqX = diff(X, x, 2) == -lambda * X; % Solve the ODEs T_sol = dsolve(eqT, T(0) == 100*x*(1-x))% Arbitrary initial condition for T X_sol = dsolve(eqX, X(0) == 0, X(1) == 10) EV0000970 · EV0000971 · EV0000972
